### 5.4 Budgets and fault codes

Lproc token parameters forward existing tokens; they neither mint a
token nor alter its validity or tuple. Binding and forwarding have no
separate budget charge (§3.7); a lifted region call inside an lproc has
the same charge and token semantics as in an oracle body. In
particular, the enclosing oracle's token is not implicitly substituted
for a nested call's explicit token.

Budget = (B, heap_limit, stack_limit, depth_limit), classical. `bennett_
budget` validates: `heap_limit ≤ layout.heap_limit_max`, `stack_limit ≤
2^S_cells`, `depth_limit < 2^Dc − 1`; for a memoryless object (§7.2)
`heap_limit = stack_limit = depth_limit = 0`. B is enforced by running
exactly B steps; heap, stack and depth are wire counters.
*(non-normative, used by §4.5)* If the reference interpreter succeeds
on x under a tuple β₀, it succeeds with the same result under every
tuple β ≥ β₀ componentwise: its only limit-dependent decisions are the
flags of `lift.alloc` and `lift.call`, false on that trajectory and
still false with larger limits, and the step bound, not reached. A
nested `lift.oracle_call` row uses the tuple of its own token, not β,
which is why §4.5 fixes those tuples exactly.

**Tokens.** A token denotes a validated tuple (B, heap_limit,
stack_limit, depth_limit) and the oracle it was validated for; a region
call uses the tuple of the token it is given, and a token MAY be
supplied to any region call of the program (the checks above depend
only on the object's layout). Tokens come from `bennett_budget` (§10;
valid until the program is unloaded) or from **`tk = budget.mint @o s h
st d`** (CC and R): `@o` names an oracle of the program (a symbol
reference, §9.2) whose object's layout the checks use, and is recorded
with the token for diagnostics only; s, h, st, d are `i64` values read
as unsigned 64-bit;
the instruction applies exactly `bennett_budget`'s checks to (s, h, st,
d) before any mutation; failure is a fault record `FAULT_UB_TRAP` (§4.9)
and no token; success produces a fresh token for that tuple, valid
until its frame is reversed or its run is CLOSED, and never
constructible from an integer. A region call given a token that is not
valid is a CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP` before any effect (a launch
argument: `E_ARG`). A minted tuple binds its invocation exactly as a
host-minted one does (§11, §12): an invocation whose closure hash,
variant, inherited controls, tuple and `params_hash` equal the
`bindings` of the object's schedule executes that schedule; any other
invocation executes under the §11 defaults; a consumer MUST NOT apply a
schedule bound to another tuple, nor substitute another tuple. In
`params_hash` a token argument is encoded as its tuple
`[B,heap,stack,depth]`. A budget is classical and fixed before region entry (§3.1), so
`budget.mint` is not a region instruction (§5.1).

```
  0 FAULT_NONE            4 FAULT_UB_TRAP            8 FAULT_EFFECT_UNMODELLED
  1 FAULT_BUDGET_STEPS    5 FAULT_UB_DIV0            9 FAULT_CALLEE
  2 FAULT_BUDGET_HEAP     6 FAULT_UB_SDIV_OVERFLOW  10 FAULT_ENTROPY_EXHAUSTED
  3 FAULT_BUDGET_DEPTH    7 FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT
```

F ≥ 4. Code 10 is recorded by the R `entropy` device (§7.3) and is also
the code a lifted oracle that virtualises entropy uses (`fault 10`) when
its entropy input is exhausted.

**Effective cause.** The literal C of `fault C` (in every tier, §4.2,
§4.9) and of `lift.faultif p C` records the cause C′ = C if C ≠ 0 and C′ =
`FAULT_UB_TRAP` (4) if C = 0;
every other row's code is a fixed nonzero constant. So no fault is ever
recorded as `FAULT_NONE`: a faulted outcome F_B(x) = (0, code) has code ≠
0, and the fault register distinguishes it from every success (F ≥ 4
makes 4 representable). Forward and inverse fault writes use C′.

### 5.5 Nesting and matched cleanup

 succeeded in 40ms:
### 7.4 Run history and the dynamic SSA model (CC, R)

The classical environment holds **dynamic instances**: every executed
defining instruction creates a new instance retained in the run history
until reversal; a name denotes its most recent instance. Frames:

| Instruction | Frame |
|---|---|
| any defining instruction | its defined instance |
| `br` | predecessor block, prior block-parameter instances |
| `store` | address, width, overwritten bytes |
| `alloc` | old bump pointer, the table entry written, the zero-filled range |
| `free` | the entry's old live bit |
| `call` | invocation boundary (callee, argument values) |
| `ret` | result values, the callee's activation and the frame link |
| `uncall` | the reversed segment's id (skipped by whole-run reversal, below) |
| `effect` | tape index plus the device-specific record of §7.3; reversal also removes its effect-tape entry |
| `hostcall` | payload bytes and reply bytes |
| `qreg.*`, `measure` | the ledger record of §3.3 (ids, class, widths, prior states, whether a free's cleanliness was verified) and the measured bits |
| `budget.mint` | the token and its tuple; reversal revokes the token |
| `oracle_call` / `action_call` | (region hash, variant, inherited control count, classical args with every token's tuple, register ids): reversed by re-application / adjoint, exactly once, by this frame |
| fault record (a failing instruction, `trap`, `fault`) | cause code (§4.9) and program point; terminal. Reversal (§10) removes the record; the failing instruction had no other effect, so the state is then the state before it |

**Logical history.** Frames and dynamic instances specify the
*logical* history. An executor MAY store it by checkpoints, deltas or
deterministic replay, provided it reconstructs the same logical frames,
instances, reversal marks and traversal order; it never re-issues an
external event (effects are replayed from the effect tape and host
replies from their frames, never requested again), never re-executes a
region call to recover its record, and never copies quantum state. T4 is
stated over the logical history. **Active and audit history.** The
*active* history is the sequence of frames not marked reversed;
reversed frames and `uncall` markers remain as immutable *audit
records*. Undoing a defining frame removes its dynamic instance from the
active environment although its audit record is retained.

**Pure frames.** A frame is **pure** iff it belongs to `br` or to a
defining instruction among `const`, the binops, `udiv..srem`, `icmp`,
`select`, `zext sext trunc`, `ptrtoint inttoptr`, `load`, `qreg.width
qreg.id qreg.from_id`: it changes only the set of instances. Every other
frame (`store alloc free call ret uncall trap fault effect hostcall
qreg.new qreg.free qreg.slice qreg.concat measure budget.mint`, region
calls, fault records) is impure.

**History traversal** (the one reversal procedure, used by segment
`uncall` and by whole-run reversal). A range of frames is **reversed**
by visiting its frames in LIFO order; a frame already marked reversed
and an `uncall` frame are **skipped**; every other frame is undone by
its row of the table above and then marked reversed. Reversed frames
stay in the history, marked, and are never undone again. An `uncall`
marker is always skipped: when a later traversal (an enclosing
segment's `uncall`, or whole-run reversal) reaches it, the frames of the
segment it names are already marked and are skipped too, so neither the
marker nor its segment is undone, re-executed or redone a second time.

**Segment uncall** (`uncall @f (args) (results)`). Let S be the most
recent completed call segment of the history (a `call` frame, its
matching `ret` frame and every frame between them). The `uncall` is
legal iff S is a call of `@f`, S's recorded boundary argument and result
values equal the operand values, **every frame after S is pure**, and S
contains, at any nesting depth, **no `measure` frame** and no `qreg.
free` frame whose cleanliness was not verified (hardware, §17 item 14);
otherwise it is a CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP` and changes nothing
beyond the fault record (§4.9). Execution reverses S by the history traversal (nested segments are
undone; nested segments already cancelled by an inner `uncall`, and that
inner `uncall` frame, are skipped), and pushes an `uncall` frame naming
S. The instances defined inside S no longer exist; using such a name
before it is redefined is a CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP`.
**Whole-run reversal** (§10) is the history traversal over the whole
history: S's effects on memory, the allocation table, devices, the
register ledger and tokens were undone by the `uncall`, and the frames between S and the
`uncall` are pure, so at the `uncall` frame every component except the
retained instances already equals its value before S. T4 is stated over
these frames. *(non-normative)* S is never re-executed and never redone;
the compute–copy–`uncall` idiom derives what it keeps through pure
instructions before the `uncall`. Suite A contains: `f` calls `g`,
uncalls `g`, returns; `g` performs one `clock.read`; `call f; uncall f`
leaves the clock at its entry value; and `uncall` of a segment that
measured is a trap.

# Mixed fat object — shared contract scope

Proposal for every revision in this directory; not a published ISA amendment.
Clause references are to [ISA 0.9](../../../spec/Bennett-ISA-Spec-0.9.md),
[the North Star](../../../../bennett/docs/Bennett-NorthStar.md), and, where
qualified, the maintained dialect 0.2/0.3 texts. This is the shared statement
required by bennett-4ht and the council rubric's standing mixed-fat-object check.

## Citable scoping statement

> A mixed fat object retains an ordinary native host binary and embeds ISA
> objects for marked device modules and their linked device closures (North
> Star §§2, 3.3, 4.4). ISA §§9.1–9.3 identify each device object and selected
> root; §12.3 certificates, owner records and reports cover that closure,
> with a separate binding/context set per device root and [] relative to
> that root (§12.1). Native entry, argv/envp and host-only libc need no ISA
> region, frame or certificate record; native comparison binds the selected
> materialised closure, not the whole host. Today §10 launches a device CC
> entry with copied arguments/entropy; §5.4 budgets bind device invocations,
> §3.3/§10 handles belong to a loaded program/run, and §7.3/§10 tapes cover
> device R effects. These require explicit container-to-object/root/certificate
> association, host-entry-to-device-entry bindings, budget/handle lifetime
> negotiation and tape/run association in a versioned mixed-launch contract.
> ISA §7.1 defines device memory but §10 defines no general host-memory-by-reference
> launch ABI: that contract must add address/layout mapping, range/liveness,
> aliasing, ownership/synchronisation, writeback and reversal rules, preserving
> dialect §§6.6, 8.5, 19.4–19.5. Until published, these are explicit
> bennett-4ht boundary obligations, not grounds to require whole-binary
> lowering or omit mixed execution.

## Present assumptions and exact publication changes

| Contract object | Assumption today | Exact change needed for mixed use |
|---|---|---|
| Object and linking (§§9.1–9.3) | One ISA object's closed sections and linked symbols; no native-container schema. | Publish a versioned inventory associating embedded/linked ISA object bytes and object_hash with selected device roots and certificates. A native-container hash, if used, is a separate identity. |
| Certificates/inventory (§§12.1–12.3) | Closure-keyed device-region records; current text does not explicitly delimit native inventory. | Insert the shared scoping rule in §12.3, cross-reference from §9.2, and require complete device-owner/link-proof coverage per root. Multiple device roots can share static closures without merging root-relative contexts. |
| Launch/entry (§10) | bennett_run(program, cc_entry, args, entropy) starts device CC; it is not native main. | Publish a host call/return shim and container/root selection/feature negotiation. Define native argument/result marshalling, including ptr (whose host binding §10 does not specify), status/fault propagation, suspend/reverse/close and ownership. Do not invent a current general launch shim. |
| Budgets (§5.4; §§10–11) | Validated token tuples and identity scoped to a program/run; action_call has no primary token (§4.7). | Bind selected device invocation budgets and token equality to the launch request; charge actual device/replay work. State host/shim accounting separately; a native process has no invented global B. |
| Handles (§3.3; §10; dialect §6.6) | Register ids refer to the loaded program's ledger; run ownership and token validity are explicit. | Map host-visible opaque handles to that program/ledger with validity and cross-launch ownership checks; preserve class vectors and token identities. Do not serialize native pointers as device ids or token tuples as identities. |
| Effect tape (§7.3; §10) | Run-associated R effects and explicit copied hostcall requests; no implicit log of native host I/O. | Associate each device run and tape with the launched root and define host effect/proxy ordering and replay at the boundary, preserving §7.4 reversal. Saved-journal interchange needs its own versioned replay profile. |
| Host memory by reference (§§7.1–7.2; dialect §§8.5, 17.1, 19.4–19.5) | Device layout plus dialect snapshot/replay and boundary-address/promotion rules; copied hostcall bytes do not provide general shared pointers. | Publish the mapping and lifetime protocol named in the statement, including allocator metadata/history and pointer-rich layouts, so ordinary host memory can be supplied safely without universal host promotion. Retain existing snapshot/replay as a construction, not the sole eventual capability. |

For example native main can call marked roots A and B in distinct ISA objects.
Each has its own object/root certificate and root context []; neither
certificate inventories main or host-only libc. A shared linked device
callee is checked in each root's actual invocation tree. A native comparison
executes the corresponding materialised region/callee closure under its
recorded layout, input and tool configuration (dialect §§17.7, 19.5, 19.9).

These notes specify boundary scope and exact remaining contract work; they
implement no container, host shim or memory registration. The existing
bennett-4ht issue tracks that publication/implementation obligation. Current
device contracts and their complete estimate/proof requirements remain in
force; an unimplemented boundary is not evidence that mixed execution works.

## 9. Objects, identity, linking, reversible ABI (object 0.9)

### 9.1 Sections and schemas

Order fixed: `symbols imports functions data devices layout schedule
certificate coverage`; each at most once; unknown sections or fields are
errors. Metadata sections are one JCS line each, an object:

```
  symbols:  { "<local index>": { "name", "kind": "cc"|"r"|"oracle"|"action"|"perm_action"|"lproc"|"data", "signature": <sig>, "interface_hash" } }
  imports:  { "<name>": { "signature": <sig>, "pinned_closure_hash"?: hex } }
  devices:  { "models": { "<dev>": { "version": "0.9" } }, "services": { "<reverse-dns id>": { "version": "…" } } }
  layout:   { "ptr_width", "H", "A_tab", "S_cells", "null_size", "heap_base", "heap_limit_max",
              "globals": { "<name>": { "base", "size", "image": "<data name>" } },
              "interface_regions": { "<oracle>": { "<region>": { "base" } } }, "block_image"?: "<data name>" }
  schedule: §11      certificate: §12      coverage: { "entries": [ §13 ] }
  <sig>:    kind "oracle": { "tier":"q", "kind", "in":[w…], "tgt":w, "fault":F, "mem":[{"A","W","role"}], "args":[types], "abi":"0.9" }
            kind "action"/"perm_action": { "tier":"q", "kind", "mut":[w…], "in":[w…], "mem":[…], "args":[types], "bounds":[…], "abi" }
            kind "lproc": { "tier":"q", "kind", "params":[w…], "result":w, "tokens":["token"…], "args":[types], "mem":[{"A","W","role":"in"}], "abi" }
            kind "cc"/"r": { "tier", "kind", "params":[types], "results":[types], "abi" }
            kind "data": { "tier":"-", "kind", "size", "abi" }
```

Integers that may exceed 2^53 are decimal strings, and a JSON number ≥
2^53 anywhere in metadata is an error. `data` lines are `NAME SP HEX`.
**Encodings.** `types` in `args`, `params` and `results` are the §16.2
type spellings as JSON strings (`"i64"`, `"ptr"`, `"f64"`, `"token"`,
`"qreg[8]"`); `w` in `in`, `tgt`, `mut`, lproc `params` and `result` is
the integer bundle or location width (§16.3). An lproc's `tokens`,
`args` and `mem` arrays are mandatory (empty arrays when absent from the
header); `args` excludes tokens and `mem` has only `in` roles (§3.7).
Array order is header order within each group. A **program-point id**
(§9.2) is the JSON array `[interface_hash, block name, ordinal]`.
Certificate `frame` fields use exactly the typed-record schema of §9.4;
unknown fields are errors there as in every metadata record.
`devices.models` lists every §7.3 model that an `effect` of the object
uses (an `effect` on an unlisted §7.3 model is a load error; a device
outside §7.3 is never listed and faults at run time, §17 item 3; a key
that is not a §7.3 model is an unknown field); `devices.services` lists
every `hostcall` service (§10). An interface region's name (its param
name and its `layout.interface_regions.<oracle>` key) MUST differ from
every symbol and import name of the object, since `@name` in `lift.addr`
denotes either (§9.2).

### 9.2 Identity

An lproc's token, classical and region parameter groups are part of
its JCS signature (§9.1), including their order and types. The header
and every forwarded operand list are canonical function bytes; changing
them changes the envelope and, through references, the caller's closure
hash. Fixed binding instances are derived per oracle (§3.7), not new
symbols or separately hashed bodies; instance rows share the source
row's program-point id and expand once per fixed row instance per step,
while the instance and semantic-call occurrence components in §11
invocation paths and §12.1 context keys distinguish their uses. [deps: ISA-3.7-P5, ISA-3.7-P6, ISA-11.1-P9, ISA-12.1-P11] Entry argument values and token tuples bind invocations through
`params_hash`, not through the lproc's interface hash (this section).
Imports must match all five groups; validation and instance formation
are repeated after linking (§9.3).

**Canonical function bytes**: the text from the function's header line
through its last instruction line, lines joined by LF, no trailing LF.
**Semantic envelope** of a symbol (byte-exact; every part ends with LF):

```
  (1) "isa=" <full version string, e.g. 0.9>
  (2) "ptr_width=" INT
  (3) the JCS signature
  (4) the canonical function bytes (with reference tokens replaced, below); for a data symbol: its data line NAME SP HEX
  (5a) for every data entry in the closure set, sorted by name bytewise:        "data " NAME " " HEX
  (5b) for every layout leaf in the closure set, sorted by JCS path bytes: "layout " <JCS key-component array> " " <JCS value>. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
  (5c) for an R/CC function, for every device model and service it uses, sorted: "device " <name> " " <JCS entry>
  (6) for every referenced symbol outside the symbol's SCC, in order of first reference: its 64-hex hash
```

**Closure set**, by symbol kind. **Oracle**: **all** `layout` leaves of
the object except `interface_regions` entries of other oracles (every
global's pre-entry affects its allocation faults), its own `interface_
regions.<oracle>.*` leaves, and every data image those leaves name.
**Lproc**: the leaves and images of every global `@g` it names in `lift.
addr`. **Action / permutation action**: no layout leaves; the `@image`
of each `qregion` is an ordinary symbol reference (replaced in part (4),
listed in part (6)). **CC / R function**: all `layout` leaves, every
data image they name, and in (5c) every device model and `hostcall`
service it uses. **Data symbol**: parts (5)–(6) are empty. A layout leaf
is a scalar or an object at the deepest level the schema lists (`globals.
<name>` and `interface_regions.<oracle>.<region>` are leaves; `ptr_
width`, `H`, … are leaves); a scalar's JCS value is its decimal text.
**Globals are data symbols**: every global `g` in `layout.globals` MUST
have `image` = `g` and the object MUST define a `data` symbol named `g`
of that size, so the token `@g` in `lift.addr` is both the global and a
symbol reference (replaced by the data symbol's hash; the global's
`base` and `size` enter through the layout leaf). An `@name` naming an
interface region (in `lift.addr`) is not a symbol reference and is left
unchanged. **Reference replacement**: in parts
(4)–(5) every `@name` symbol-reference token is replaced by `@` followed
by the referenced symbol's 64-hex hash (the symbol's own name in its
header line is a definition, not a reference, and is left unchanged); the **interface hash** uses
interface hashes of defined symbols and `SHA-256(name ‖ 0x00 ‖ JCS
signature)` (hex) for unresolved imports; the **closure hash** uses
closure hashes. **Acyclic and cyclic symbols.** The reference graph
has an edge from a symbol to every symbol it references (parts
(4)–(5)). An **acyclic** symbol — a one-member SCC that does not
reference itself — has hash = SHA-256 of its own envelope (example 1's
`inc8`, example 2's `zero` and `sum_db`); the SCC procedure below applies
to every **cyclic** SCC, including a one-member SCC whose function
references itself (example 2's `sum`). **SCCs**: members ordered by name bytewise; every intra-
SCC `@name` token (parts (4)–(5)) is replaced by `@$<decimal index>`;
intra-SCC references contribute no part-(6) line; `scc_hash` = SHA-256
over the concatenation of the members' envelopes in that order; member
hash = SHA-256 over the ASCII bytes of `<scc_hash hex>:<decimal index>`.
**object_hash** = SHA-256 of the canonical object text with the
`schedule`, `certificate` and `coverage` sections deleted together with
the blank line preceding each. `reference_program_hash` = the oracle's
interface hash. `params_hash` = SHA-256 of the JCS array of classical
parameter values (the typed encoding of §12.3, K13); `schedule_hash` =
SHA-256 of the schedule's JCS bytes.
**Program-point id** = `(symbol interface hash, block name, instruction
ordinal within the block counting nested lines in text order)`.
Executable certificates and caches bind to **closure hashes** (required,
§12).

A layout leaf's path is the JCS array of its original JSON key
components from the layout root to the deepest schema-listed leaf,
so a scalar has a one-component path and an interface-region leaf has
path `["interface_regions","<oracle>","<region>"]`.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
Part (5b) emits exactly one line for each structured leaf in the closure
set, including distinct leaves whose dotted renderings coincide, ordered
by the unsigned UTF-8 bytes of that JCS path array, independently of the
leaf values; no dotted rendering is used as a key or an ordering key.
[deps: ISA-9.1-P1, ISA-16.1]
An oracle's own interface-region leaves and entries of other oracles
are selected by equality of the original JSON key under
`interface_regions` to the oracle's name, never by a prefix of a
rendered path; an lproc's global leaves are selected by equality of
the key under `globals` to the global name.
[deps: ISA-3.7-P1, ISA-9.1-P1]

**Envelope boundary lemma** (non-normative).
For a function symbol, §16.2 requires a header beginning `fn `,
`oracle `, `action `, `perm_action ` or `lproc `; following lines are
`regs`, block labels, instructions, or structured closing braces. [deps: ISA-16.2]
No §4 opcode is `data`, `layout` or `device`; a result with one of
those names has ` = ` after the name, which cannot begin any part-(5)
line (a data name, a JSON array, or a device name is required there). [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
No canonical function line is a bare 64-hex string, so the first
part-(5)/(6) line ends the function bytes unambiguously. [deps: ISA-4.8, ISA-16.2]
The fixed `data `, `layout `, `device ` prefixes distinguish (5a–c),
and a bare 64-hex line distinguishes (6); JSON strings escape LF. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1]
For a data symbol, the signature kind selects exactly one data line
for (4), with (5)–(6) empty, even when the data name itself is `data`,
`layout` or `device`. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.2]
Together with the fixed first three lines and the injective JCS array
paths, these facts make the envelope parts recoverable without length
prefixes; this is an encoding argument, not a claim of SHA-256 injectivity. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-16.1, ISA-16.2, ISA-9.2-P4]

### 9.3 Linking

suggestions: []
In a mixed fat object, 'loaded program' means a loaded device program instance, not the native host process or entire fat binary. The native host may retain that instance and repeatedly invoke its device CC entries; the specified memory, allocation-table, bump-pointer and register-ledger persistence applies to that instance, with separate state for separate loaded instances. Loading a device program or closing or reversing a device run does not initialize or roll back unrelated native host state. This rule does not require moving native main, argv/envp handling or host-only libc into the device object. Later-entry certificate replay records the actual device entry-state preparation assumptions, without presuming a fresh native process.

 succeeded in 161ms:
# adg — loaded-program memory lifetime across launches

Proposal for council review; `b-isa-spec-adg`.

## Defect and North Star scope

ISA 0.9 §7.1 defines initial memory, allocation table and bump pointer
without saying whether initialization occurs at load or every run.
§3.2 places the register ledger at program scope; §10 gives a run
exclusive ownership only until CLOSED; §7.4 and T4 restore a run's entry
state on eligible reversal. These strongly favor program-lifetime state
but do not explicitly settle classical memory initialization.

Dialect 0.3 draft §§6.5–6.6 uses a persistent qreg table in device memory
and requires parameter/result roots to survive entry calls. North Star
§3.1's register handles and §5's REPL/library device model require
cross-entry quantum state. A fresh-every-run interpretation loses the
table while live registers remain. This is a contract ambiguity with
observable results, S0 under C1/C5; persistence is the compatible reading.
It adds no restriction to the North Star, including repeated SQLite or
kernel invocations.

## Alternatives and hand-worked witness

Layout: ptr_width=16, H=16, A_tab=2, S_cells=0, null_size=1,
heap_base=16, heap_limit_max=16; a one-byte global g at base 1 with image
00. Run A allocates one byte (returns 16) and stores 7 into g, then
completes and is CLOSED without reversal. Run B loads g and allocates
one byte. Under persistence it returns `(7,17)`; under reinitialization
it returns `(0,16)`. These are distinct visible results from the same
loaded program. Freeing 16 does not reclaim storage under §7.1.

If A is instead successfully REVERSED before CLOSED, its allocation
and store are undone, and B returns `(0,16)`. Merely closing A does not
reverse it. An eligible failed run retains its executed prefix until it
is reversed; closing a failed run does not silently roll it back.

| Alternative | Assessment |
|---|---|
| Program-lifetime memory and ledger (recommended) | Matches §10 ownership and the dialect's persistent table. |
| Reinitialize on each run | Would need a different host-held table and an explicit reset/lifetime ABI; it changes existing consumer assumptions. |
| Implicit reset when no register is live | Still changes classical allocation/global results; no clause authorizes it. |

## Recommended exact spec diff

Insert after §7.1's Initial state sentence:

```diff
+That state is established once when the program is loaded. Classical
+memory, allocation-table entries and the bump pointer belong to the
+loaded program and persist across runs. For sequential launches, a new
+run starts from the state left by the preceding CLOSED run. Closing a run does not restore its
+entry state. If the preceding run was successfully REVERSED before
+being CLOSED, its reversible state is its restored entry state (§7.4,
+§10, T4). A FAILED run otherwise leaves its executed prefix in place.
+Register-ledger entries and live register identities likewise persist
+under §3.3; ownership ends at CLOSED, not the register lifetime. Unloading
+and loading a program creates a new program instance with initial state;
+register ids of the old instance are not imported into it.
```

Append to §10's ownership paragraph:

```diff
+Persistence does not relax exclusive ownership of touched registers or
+memory while a run is not CLOSED. Separate loaded program instances have
+separate state; initialization is never repeated merely to start a run.
```

All examples here use sequential CLOSED runs. This wording does not add a
blanket prohibition on disjoint concurrent runs or invent a memory merge
rule. The existing ownership restrictions still govern overlapping uses.

## Hashes, catalog and consumers

This pins an existing reading: no source, envelope, target or catalog byte
changes and no hash re-pin. A consumer that reset memory on each run must
change its load/run lifecycle; plugin qreg-table code should not change.
Launch bindings and certificates that replay a later entry must include
its actual entry-state/preparation assumptions rather than presume a
fresh load. Domain claims do not acquire coverage of all histories merely
by showing one initialized run. Header diagnostics already include
E_INVALID_STATE; no new numeric status is proposed.

## Conformance vectors and verification

Test load→A→CLOSED→B and load→A→REVERSED→CLOSED→B with the results
above; check every byte, table live bit, bump pointer, register identity
and token lifetime. Add a failed A whose prefix stored 7: B sees 7 unless
that prefix was reversed. If B passes a register still owned by A before
A is CLOSED, it must return E_INVALID_STATE before effects. Fresh loading
restores g=0 and bump=16. A stored id can be recovered by qreg.from_id in
the next run after ownership is released. These are hand derivations;
the scalar state transitions are checked by
[contract-verification](2026-11-02-contract-verification.md), not by a
new reference execution.

## Release and after acceptance

Recommend a **0.9 K wording repair** to §§7.1/10 with lifecycle vectors.
Council should explicitly accept program scope under existing ownership, then
VM/reference owners validate their lifecycle and the dialect updates its
cross-reference. Keep adg open until the contract decision lands.

/// One-shot §10 launch: bennett_load (object parsed and hash-checked by the
/// caller), positional bennett_run of a device CC entry with copied
/// arguments and entropy, HOST_REQUEST/reply through the checked reply
/// queue, bennett_result_* reporting, optional bennett_reverse, and
/// bennett_run_close. Paths that need staged deliverables 8-11 (PROGRESS)
/// fail by name before any run is reported.
fn run_program(program: &Object, name: &str, options: CcOptions) -> Result<Json> {

        return Err(fail(
            "§10 bennett_load: a target description cannot be honoured yet (staged deliverable 9: §8 target descriptions, suite E; PROGRESS.md deliverables 8-11); no run is reported",
        ));
    }

        return Err(fail(
            "§10 bennett_load: the object's schedule cannot be executed verbatim yet (staged deliverable 10: §11 schedule consumer, suite H; PROGRESS.md deliverables 8-11); no run is reported",
        ));
    }

        .functions
        .get(name)
        .ok_or_else(|| earg(format!("unknown CC entry @{name}")))?;
    if f.kind != "cc" {
        return Err(earg(format!(
            "entry @{name} has kind {}; a launch executes a device CC entry",
            f.kind
        )));
    }

    let budgets = budgets_json(&args);
    let mut machine = ClassicalMachine::with_quantum_registers_and_globals(
        program,
        name,
        &args,
        &options.entropy,
        registers,
        &options.mem,
    )?;
    for draw in &options.draws {
        machine.push_measurement_draw(*draw)?;
    }
    let entry = machine.snapshot();
    let entry_digest = machine.memory_digest()?;
    let mut replies = load_replies(options.replies.as_deref())?;
    let steps = drive(
        &mut machine,
        &mut replies,
        options.fuel,
        None,
        None,
        tape.as_mut(),
    )?;
    check_replies_consumed(&replies)?;
    let outcome = machine.outcome();
    let failed = outcome.fault.is_some();
    // §10: a FAILED run exposes no values; its fault records are the result.
    let results = if failed {
        Json::Array(Vec::new())
    } else {
        values_json(&outcome.values)?
    };
    let forward_tape = tape_json(&machine)?;
    let forward_requests = host_requests_json(&machine)?;
    let forward_measurements = measurements_json(&machine);
### 6.5 Realisation on ISA 0.9: direct values, static files and the qreg table

A `qreg` is not storable in memory, but its id is: `qreg.id` reads it, `qreg.from_id` returns an alias
and `qreg.width` reads the run-time width (ISA §3.3, K1). CC and R code hold `qreg` values in their classical
environment (dynamic SSA instances, block parameters, call arguments and results; ISA §3.2, §3.3, §7.4). The plugin
realises each allocation site s (a `qalloc` or `qborrow` call) in one of three ways:

- **Direct.** If no `ROOT(s)` atom reaches a memory slot (PT7, PT8), the handle is a CC `qreg`
  value threaded through SSA values, block parameters, call arguments and results
  (ISA §3.3, E11). Every execution of s creates a new dynamic instance,
  so loops and recursion need no bound.
- **Static register file.** Otherwise, when N(s) is finite, the plugin MAY use the qreg table below or a static file: a physical **slot** is a pair (s, instance
  index k < N(s)); ROOT(s) is static provenance, not run-time identity. For
  each slot the plugin keeps one CC `qreg` variable, threaded like a direct handle through every
  CC variant that may use it; `qalloc` at s picks a free instance index at run time and executes
  `qreg.new cls n` into that variable (one variable per member of the width dispatch domain,
  §3.4). The default CC table holds each slot's current generation tag and liveness;
  a fresh-tag allocator gives every allocation a new identity (§6.1), stored in memory.
  A marker operand is dispatched by matching its full tag against current live candidate
  slots (compare tree, §14.2); a missing or retired generation traps T-DEAD before
  changing any register. Free retires the identity even if the slot is immediately reused.
  Recycle tags only after old copies are proven unobservable, never by slot reuse alone.
  This table and allocator use ordinary classical operations, without qreg.id.
  N(s) bounds simultaneous physical slots, not the number of historical identities. A direct handle that is converted to an integer
  (`ptrtoint`, a store) gets a tag in the same way.
- **Qreg table.** When N(s) = ∞ the site MUST use the qreg table. Memory-reaching handles with no finite reference instance bound MUST use a persistent lossless tag-to-ledger-id mapping and qreg.from_id at use, preserving pointer-width tags, identity, liveness, ownership and checked widths. [deps: DLT-6.1-P1, DLT-6.4-P1, DLT-6.5-P10, ISA-3.3-P7, ISA-3.3-P13, ISA-3.3-P14, ISA-3.3-P19, ISA-10]
  The **qreg table** is a plugin-added global `__bennett_rt_qtab` of `B_tab` bytes, sized by
  `--bennett-qreg-table-bytes` (D29), with the placement and complete zero data image of §19.5.
  Its header occupies `C_tab = align_up(ceil(pw/8),8)` bytes and holds a little-endian pw-bit
  **issued-tag count** c, initially 0 (header padding is zero). It has
  `Q_tab = floor((B_tab - C_tab)/16)` records; the option MUST cover the header.
  Record v, for tags 1 through `Q_tab`, starts at byte `C_tab + 16·(v−1)` and contains
  an `i64` id followed by an `i64` state (0 unassigned, 1 live, 2 retired), little-endian;
  unused tail bytes are zero. The next fresh tag is c+1. Tag values are 1, 2, … and are
  never reused, which satisfies §6.1. Device classical code reads and writes the table with
  ordinary `load`/`store`, so R history reverses it (ISA §7.4). The operations are:
  - `qalloc`: check `T-SIZE`; if c has reached `Q_tab`, code 2 before any effect; `q = qreg.new cls n`; `id = qreg.id q`; record (id, live) at tag c+1 and increment c. The handle value is the tag.
  - use of a marker operand with tag v: if v is 0 or greater than c, or its state is not live, `T-DEAD` before any effect; `q = qreg.from_id id`, then for an indexed gate check i below `qreg.width q` (`T-INDEX`) and `s = qreg.slice q i 1` with the run-time `i` (ISA E12), or transfer q to the fixed-width slot with the ISA's width check. A `from_id` that fails because another run that is not CLOSED owns the register is a CC trap / R fault with code 4 (ISA §3.3, §10), never a silent retry.
  - `qfree` of tag v: as a use, then `qreg.free q` (`T-CLEAN` on a verifying executor, ISA §3.3) and state retired.
  - a register that enters through a `QREG` parameter or a call result and reaches memory gets a tag by **interning**: the id's existing live tag if the table has one, else, after the same capacity check, record (id, live) at tag c+1 and increment c. One register has at most one live tag, so §6.3 equality of tags is identity. The reference lookup is a linear scan of the table; any structure with the same results is permitted.
  The helper code is not a counted materialised instruction (§17.6) and its accesses are outside the native comparison (§17.7), which holds no quantum state. An oracle or lifted variant never reads the table: handles reach regions only through CC or R region calls (§8.4).

**Reference instance bound** of a site s in function F with a static register file:

Before rules 1–2, augment each function's CFG with N29 continuation edges:
from every call whose closure may longjmp, add an edge to each setjmp continuation
in every function that may be an ancestor of that call (including the same function).
Include the materialised __bennett_rt_setjmp continuations, candidate callees and
interprocedural call edges. The reference default remains conservative: if a setjmp
continuation in any may-ancestor A of F (A=F included) can reach s through these CFG
and call edges, s is not self-disjoint and E(s)=∞. Apply this rule to §7.5(b) R
R allocation as well. Such a site uses the qreg table, never rejection; ISA K1 supplies
qreg.width/id/from_id and K2 their execution in R. No witness claims a finite reference bound.

**Entry-boundary liveness (before rules 1–2).** If an instance of s may be
live at the return of any device entry that may reach s — memory live at that
return (a global, heap object, or memory reachable from a result or env) may
hold ROOT(s), and not every path from s through the augmented CFGs and call
edges to that return passes a qfree with provenance exactly {s} — then s is
not self-disjoint and E(s)=∞. Use the qreg table,
never rejection. The host may call entries any number of times: executions per
entry call do not bound instances that survive return. Under §7.5(b), substitute
reversible-invocation return; the same boundary test applies.
An internal callee's return alone is not a device-entry return: a guaranteed
free in its caller before the entry returns can discharge this condition.

**Parameter and result roots.** Apply the same boundary test to candidate handle
parameter ROOT(E,i) (§6.6), and to a qalloc instance returned as an entry QREG
result. Persistent memory (a global, heap object, or memory reachable from result
or env) or the entry's QREG result may retain it. Include every reusable exit,
including unwind/resume edges, in the augmented interprocedural graph; an exact-root
qfree on every path from receipt/allocation discharges liveness. Otherwise E=∞:
the host may supply or retain unboundedly many distinct live registers across calls.
Marker uses of such roots loaded from persistent memory use the qreg table by interning,
never rejection. Persistent parameter and result roots MUST resolve through the program ledger across entry calls without an entry-count or static-file bound, while retaining the ISA's exclusive open-run ownership and same-id reversal rules. [deps: DLT-6.5-P6, DLT-6.5-P7, DLT-6.6, ISA-3.3-P7, ISA-3.3-P14, ISA-3.3-P18, ISA-10]
A handle parameter used directly only within its receiving entry
keeps the direct realisation, as does a returned QREG never stored in memory.
The infinite bound on a returned root is relevant to its static-file realisation
when it reaches memory; it does not impose a finite bound on direct QREG transport. Under §7.5(b), substitute reversible-invocation exit
for receipt through env; the table operations are the same in R (ISA K2).

1. s is **self-disjoint** iff the only memory its `ROOT(s)` atom reaches is allocas of F whose
   address does not escape F (it is not stored, returned, or passed to a call other than a marker
   call, which includes the `env` of a region-entry marker), and every CFG path of F from s to a `ret`, to s again, or to a call
   whose callee may reach F through the call graph passes through a `qfree` whose operand's
### 6.6 Host–device boundary

A device entry's parameters and results of handle type bind to launch `QREG` arguments and
results (ISA §10). A `ptr` parameter of a device entry is a **handle parameter** iff its
candidate atom `ROOT(E, i)` (added by PT11 to every pointer parameter of every device entry)
reaches a marker handle operand; otherwise the candidate atom is removed before §6.2 is checked.
A handle parameter binds to `QREG`; host code may copy, store and compare launch register ids and pass
them back. Leak checking happens at program end and at `bennett_qreg_free`, not at each
device-entry return, which gives the REPL's lexical lifetime (NS §5; audit B-30). *(non-normative)*
A register returned to the host stays live across launches until the host frees it or a
`measure` consumes it (ISA §10: "register ids created by the run remain valid unless measured
or freed"). QREG parameters and results MUST retain their register identity across entries and may outlive a device-entry return, with persistent lookup subject to the ISA run-ownership and CLOSED rules and leak checking at program end. [deps: DLT-6.5-P7, DLT-6.6-P1, ISA-3.3-P14, ISA-3.3-P20, ISA-3.3-P21, ISA-10]

### 6.7 Copy, XOR and in-place rules

Copying a handle copies a reference. Quantum data moves only by:

- `qload(dst, mem, addr)`: XOR of cell `addr` of `mem` into `dst` (§18.3); a copy when `dst` is
  clean, the source preserved;
- `qstore(mem, addr, val)`: XOR of `val` into cell `addr` of `mem` (ISA `qstore`), whatever `mem`
  holds;
- `qprep(h, src, n)`: XOR of classical bytes into `h` (§3.2);
- `inplace(g, ginv, budget, desc)`: x ← g(x), admitted only under §8.7;
- `oracle(f, budget, desc)`: targets ⊕= outputs, fault ⊕= code, inputs preserved (§8).

/// operation transferred (console output, clock bytes, entropy bytes, or a
/// 512-byte sector), recorded as the effect executed.
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub struct TapeEntry {
    pub dev: String,
    pub op: String,
    pub args: Vec<u128>,
    pub bytes: Retained<u8>,
}
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Default)]
struct Devices {
    console: Retained<u8>,
    clock: u64,
    entropy_position: usize,
    blocks: BTreeMap<u128, Vec<u8>>,
    tape: Retained<TapeEntry>,
}
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub struct ClassicalSnapshot {
    instances: crate::instances::Instances,
    memory: Memory,
    devices: Devices,

/// One changed dynamic instance between two snapshots: the top instance of
/// `(activation, name)` before and after (either side absent when the
/// instance does not exist there, §7.4).
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq)]
pub struct RegisterDelta {
    pub activation: u64,
    pub name: String,
    pub pre: Option<ClassicalValue>,
    pub post: Option<ClassicalValue>,
}
/// The register and memory-byte changes between two snapshots; allocation,
/// device and handle changes are reported by `first_difference`, not here.
#[derive(Clone, Debug, PartialEq, Default)]
pub struct SnapshotDelta {
    pub registers: Vec<RegisterDelta>,
    /// (address, before, after) for every byte whose value changed.
    pub memory: Vec<(u128, u8, u8)>,
}
impl ClassicalSnapshot {
    /// The active §7.3 tape at this checkpoint. An observer can compare the
    /// newly appended entry before a step is published; `uncall` can shrink it.
pub struct ClassicalMachine<'a> {
    object: &'a Object,
    state: ClassicalSnapshot,
    history: Vec<HistoryFrame>,
    segments: Vec<Segment>,
    entropy: Vec<u8>,
    initial: ClassicalSnapshot,
    measurement_draws: VecDeque<f64>,
    measurement_rng: [u64; 4],
    idle_registers: Vec<u64>,
}

impl<'a> ClassicalMachine<'a> {
    pub fn new(
        object: &'a Object,
        entry: &str,
        args: &[ClassicalValue],
        entropy: &[u8],
    ) -> Result<Self> {
        Self::construct(object, entry, args, entropy, QuantumRegisters::new(), &[])
    }
    fn construct(
        object: &'a Object,
        entry: &str,
        args: &[ClassicalValue],
        entropy: &[u8],
        registers: QuantumRegisters,
        globals: &[(String, Vec<u8>)],
    ) -> Result<Self> {
        object.ensure_linked()?;
        let f = object
            .functions
            .get(entry)
            .filter(|f| matches!(f.kind.as_str(), "cc" | "r"))
            .ok_or_else(|| Error::new("§3.4: classical entry not found".into()))?;
        if args.len() != f.params[0].len()
            || args
                .iter()
                .zip(&f.params[0])
                .any(|(a, p)| !a.matches(&p.ty, object.ptr_width))
        {
            return err("§10", "classical entry argument type/count mismatch");
        }
        for (p, a) in f.params[0].iter().zip(args) {
            if let (Type::Qreg(n), ClassicalValue::Qreg(id)) = (&p.ty, a) {
                if registers.bundle(*id)?.len() as u64 != *n {
                    return err("§10", "E_ARG: qreg argument width differs");
                }
            }
        }
        let mut instances = crate::instances::Instances::default();
        for (p, v) in f.params[0].iter().zip(args) {
            instances.push((0, p.name.clone()), v.clone());
        }
        let mut devices = Devices::default();
        if let Some(l) = object.section("layout") {
            if let Some(image) = l.object()?.get("block_image") {
                let data = object.data.get(image.string()?).ok_or_else(|| {
                    Error::new("§7.3/§9.1: block_image names undefined data".into())
                })?;
                if !data.len().is_multiple_of(512) {
                    return err("§7.3", "block image must have complete 512-byte sectors");
                }
                for (i, sector) in data.as_chunks::<512>().0.iter().enumerate() {
                    devices.blocks.insert(i as u128, sector.to_vec());
                }
            }
        }
        let mut memory = Memory::initial(object, f, &BTreeMap::new())?;
        // Caller-seeded global images (§7.1 entry state): each named global's
        // bytes replace its layout image, at exactly its declared size. The
        // seeded state is the run's entry state for reversal.
        let mut seeded = std::collections::BTreeSet::new();
        for (name, bytes) in globals {
            if !seeded.insert(name) {
                return err("§7.1", format!("global {name} seeded twice"));
            }
            let layout = object
                .section("layout")
                .ok_or_else(|| Error::new("§7.1: seeded globals require a layout".into()))?;
            let global = layout
                .get("globals")?
                .object()?
                .get(name)
                .ok_or_else(|| Error::new(format!("§7.1: unknown global {name}")))?;
            let base = global.get("base")?.uint()?;
            let size = global.get("size")?.uint()?;
            if size != bytes.len() as u128 {
                return err(
                    "§7.1",
                    format!(
                        "global {name} has size {size}, seeded with {} bytes",
                        bytes.len()
                    ),
                );
            }
            for (i, b) in bytes.iter().enumerate() {
                let address = base
                    .checked_add(i as u128)
                    .ok_or_else(|| Error::new("§7.1: global address overflow".into()))?;
                memory.set_byte(address, *b)?;
            }
        }
        let state = ClassicalSnapshot {
            instances,
            memory,
            devices,
            control: vec![Activation {
                id: 0,
                function: entry.into(),
                block: 0,
                ip: 0,
                bindings: Vec::new(),
                segment: None,
            }],
            next_id: 1,
            fault: None,
            finished: None,
            pending: None,
            quantum: registers,
        };
        Ok(Self {
            object,
            state: state.clone(),
            history: Vec::new(),
            segments: Vec::new(),
            entropy: entropy.into(),
            initial: state,
            measurement_draws: VecDeque::new(),
            measurement_rng: [1, 2, 3, 4],
            idle_registers: Vec::new(),
            .id;
        crate::reserve_more(&mut undo.definitions, 1, "§7.4", "dynamic instance history")?;
        self.define_at(id, name, value, undo);
        Ok(())
    }
    fn define_at(&mut self, id: u64, name: &str, value: ClassicalValue, undo: &mut Undo) {
        let key = (id, name.into());
        self.state.instances.push(key.clone(), value);
        undo.definitions.push(key);
    }
    fn save_bytes(&self, p: u128, len: usize, undo: &mut Undo) -> Result<()> {
        let bytes = undo
            .bytes
            .prepare(len, "§10/§7.1/§7.4", "retained memory bytes")?;
        for n in 0..len {
            bytes.push((p + n as u128, self.state.memory.byte(p + n as u128)));
        }
        Ok(())
    }
    fn undo_record(&mut self, undo: Undo) -> Result<()> {
        if !undo.bytes.is_empty() {
            self.state
                .memory
                .bytes
                .prepare(undo.bytes.iter().filter(|(_, b)| *b != 0).count())?;
        }
        for record in undo.registers.into_iter().rev() {
            self.state.quantum.undo(record)?;
        }
        if let Some(record) = undo.region {
            self.execute_region(&record, true)?;
            self.state
                .quantum
                .state
                .restore_next_wire_id(record.wire_next)?;
        }
        for key in undo.definitions.into_iter().rev() {
            if !self.state.instances.pop(&key) {
                return err("§7.4", "missing retained dynamic instance");
            }
        }
        for &(p, b) in undo.bytes.iter().rev() {
            self.state.memory.set_byte(p, b)?;
        }
        if let Some((used, len)) = undo.allocation {
            self.state.memory.heap_used = used;
            self.state.memory.allocations.truncate(len);
        }
        if let Some((n, live)) = undo.free {
            self.state.memory.allocations[n].live = live;
        }
        if undo.tape {
            let len = self.state.devices.tape.len();
            if len == 0 {
                return err("§7.3", "empty effect tape on reversal");
            }
            self.state.devices.tape.truncate(len - 1);
        }
        if let Some(d) = undo.device {
            match d {
                DeviceUndo::Console(n) => self.state.devices.console.truncate(n),
                DeviceUndo::Clock(c) => self.state.devices.clock = c,
                DeviceUndo::Entropy(p) => self.state.devices.entropy_position = p,
                DeviceUndo::Block(n, Some(v)) => {
                    self.state.devices.blocks.insert(n, v);
                }
                DeviceUndo::Block(n, None) => {
                    self.state.devices.blocks.remove(&n);
                }
            }
        }
        self.state.control = undo.control;
        self.state.next_id = undo.next_id;
        self.state.fault = undo.fault;
        self.state.finished = undo.finished;
        self.state.pending = undo.pending;
        Ok(())
    }
    fn undo_frame(&mut self, n: usize) -> Result<()> {
        if self.history[n].reversed || self.history[n].marker {
            return Ok(());
        }
        let undo = self.history[n].undo.clone();
        self.undo_record(undo)?;
        self.history[n].reversed = true;
        Ok(())
    }
    fn effect(&mut self, op: &str, args: &[String], undo: &mut Undo) -> Result<Option<u128>> {
        let Some((dev, operation)) = op.split_once('.') else {
            return Ok(Some(8));
        };
        let values = args
            .iter()
            .map(|s| self.bits(s))
            .collect::<Result<Vec<_>>>()?;
        let arity = match (dev, operation) {
            ("console", "write") | ("entropy", "read") | ("block", "read" | "write") => 2,
            ("clock", "read") => 1,
            _ => return Ok(Some(8)),
        };
        if values.len() != arity {
            return err("§7.3", "device operation arity mismatch");
        }
        let (p, len) = match dev {
            "clock" => (values[0], 8),
            "block" => (values[1], 512),
            _ => (values[0], values[1]),
        };
        if !self.state.memory.valid(p, len) {
            return Ok(Some(7));
        }
        let len = usize::try_from(len).map_err(|_| {
            Error::host_resource(
                "§7.3: host limit: device length exceeds host address space".into(),
            )
        })?;
        if dev == "entropy"
            && self
                .state
                .devices
                .entropy_position
                .checked_add(len)
                .is_none_or(|end| end > self.entropy.len())
        {
            return Ok(Some(10));
        }
        crate::reserve_more(
            self.state
                .devices
                .tape
                .prepare(0, "§7.3/§7.4", "effect tape")?,
            1,
            "§7.3/§7.4",
            "effect tape entry",
        )?;
        let transferred = match (dev, operation) {
            ("console", "write") => {
                let bytes = self.transfer_bytes(p, len, "§7.3")?;
                let old_len = self.state.devices.console.len();
                self.state
                    .devices
                    .console
                    .prepare(len, "§7.3", "console bytes")?
                    .extend_from_slice(&bytes);
                undo.device = Some(DeviceUndo::Console(old_len));
                bytes
            }
            ("clock", "read") => {
                self.save_bytes(p, 8, undo)?;
                let c = self.state.devices.clock;
                undo.device = Some(DeviceUndo::Clock(c));
                self.state.memory.store_u128(p, 64, c as u128)?;
                self.state.devices.clock = c.wrapping_add(1);
                c.to_le_bytes().to_vec()
            }
            ("entropy", "read") => {
                self.save_bytes(p, len, undo)?;
                let start = self.state.devices.entropy_position;
                let mut bytes = crate::reserved(len, "§7.3", "entropy transfer bytes")?;
                bytes.extend_from_slice(&self.entropy[start..start + len]);
                self.state.memory.bytes.prepare_write(p, &bytes)?;
                undo.device = Some(DeviceUndo::Entropy(start));
                for n in 0..len {
                    self.state
                        .memory
                        .set_byte(p + n as u128, self.entropy[start + n])?;
                }
                self.state.devices.entropy_position += len;
                bytes
            }
            ("block", "read") => {
                self.save_bytes(p, 512, undo)?;
                let bytes = self
                    .state
                    .devices
                    .blocks
                    .get(&values[0])
                    .cloned()
                    .unwrap_or_else(|| vec![0; 512]);
                self.state.memory.bytes.prepare_write(p, &bytes)?;
                for (n, b) in bytes.iter().enumerate() {
                    self.state.memory.set_byte(p + n as u128, *b)?;
                }
                bytes
            }
            ("block", "write") => {
                let sector = values[0];
                let old = self.state.devices.blocks.get(&sector).cloned();
                undo.device = Some(DeviceUndo::Block(sector, old));
                let bytes: Vec<_> = (0..512).map(|n| self.state.memory.byte(p + n)).collect();
                self.state.devices.blocks.insert(sector, bytes.clone());
                bytes
            }
            _ => {
                return err(
                    "§7.3",
                    format!("unknown device operation {dev}.{operation}"),
                )
            }
        };
        self.state
            .devices

## 0. Changes from 0.8

Every change from the accepted draft 0.8 has one entry below; nothing
changes without an entry. **Kind**: *E* = editorial (wording, or a
reading pinned that a conformant 0.8 implementation could already take,
with local semantics unchanged); *S* = semantic (some outcome or
well-formedness verdict differs from some reading of 0.8, or a
capability is added). **Hash**: every entry rides on the version bump V,
which changes envelope part (1) and every signature's `abi`, hence the
hash of **every** symbol (§9.2): the four 0.8 example hashes all change
and §16.3 republishes their objects (the 0.8 vectors remain valid 0.8
vectors); "+bytes" marks an entry that also changes the canonical bytes
of functions that use the affected form; "new" marks new symbols.
Sources: *EN* = an item of the evidence note
`docs/notes/2026-10-29-isa-09-evidence.md`; *bd* = a bead of this
repository; *SQ n* = entry n of `reference-interpreter/SPEC-QUESTIONS.md`
(its reading is adopted unless the entry says otherwise); *RC* = the
reference-interpreter council round 1
(`docs/council/isa-ref-2026-10-29/round-1/report.md` in `bennett`),
member and finding; *r8* = a council round-8 suggestion (bead
`b-isa-spec-zsw.N`); *DL* = the dialect 0.2 outline's §25 list.

**No narrowing.** No entry rejects, faults, declares inert or removes a
capability that 0.8 permits or the north star promises. The entries
that add or sharpen a well-formedness check (E11's static view rule,
E16.9, E17.2, E17.3, E17.5's unmatched uncalls, E17.8, E17.9, E17.10,
E17.12, E17.14, the block terminators of K5) concern spellings that
always have a legal equivalent or uses that 0.8 did not define soundly
(E17.14: a location value as a classical argument would be superposed);
the run-time checks (E11, K1's width checks, K3's token validity, K4's
inverse-pair check) concern operations that 0.8 did not define or
already required (0.8's `qinplace` already demanded its certificates;
K4 admits library certificates as well as exhaustive claims, as the
north star §3.1 requires).

### 4.9 Classical faults (CC, R)

A **CC trap** and an **R fault record** are one event. The failing
instruction is validated before any mutation and has no effect except
appending one **terminal fault record** (its cause code and program
point, §7.4); the run ends `FAILED` (§10) with no further instruction
executed, so the CC caller of a faulting R call executes no successor
instruction and receives no results, and a `FAILED` run has exactly
one fault record. Implicit trap causes and explicit faults have the
following code mapping:

| Cause | Code |
|---|---|
| `udiv urem sdiv srem` with divisor 0 | 5 `FAULT_UB_DIV0` |
| `sdiv srem` of the signed minimum by −1 | 6 `FAULT_UB_SDIV_OVERFLOW` |
| `load`/`store` outside every live allocation; `free` with no live unpinned entry at its address (§7.1); a `hostcall` range not in one live allocation (§10) | 7 `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` |
| `alloc` with the heap or the allocation table exhausted (§7.1); a `qreg.new` the device cannot satisfy (§3.3) | 2 `FAULT_BUDGET_HEAP` |
| an `effect` memory operand range not in one live allocation (§7.3) | 7 `FAULT_UB_MEMFAULT` |
| `effect` on a device outside §7.3 (§17 item 3) | 8 `FAULT_EFFECT_UNMODELLED` |
| `entropy.read` past the end of the tape (§7.3) | 10 `FAULT_ENTROPY_EXHAUSTED` |
| `trap`; an illegal `uncall` (§7.4); a use without a current instance (§3.4); a failed `qreg` precondition, width check or overlap check (§3.3), including an unclean `qreg.free` a verifying executor detects; an invalid token or a failed `budget.mint` validation (§5.4); a `qinplace` without a covering inverse-pair entry (§4.5) | 4 `FAULT_UB_TRAP` |
| `fault C` | C′, the effective cause of C (§5.4) |
| exhaustion of the executor's own classical resources | §17 item 16 |

`fault C` takes a literal 0 ≤ C < 2^64 (a launch fault code is a
`uint64_t`, §10); codes above 10 are program-defined. Its frame is the
fault record. `bennett_result_fault` returns the record's code
unchanged, including every nonzero program-defined code up to 2^64 − 1;
it MUST NOT replace such a code with `FAULT_UB_TRAP` or `FAULT_CALLEE`.
`trap` reports only code 4; it cannot stand in for an arbitrary `fault C`.
No host service is needed to report C (`hostcall` remains CC-only).

**Charge and history.** A CC/R `fault C` executes as one classical
instruction, taking `classical_step_time` (§8.2); it does not consume a Q
oracle's B. An R budget supplied by source instrumentation is enforced
by that code, not by the Q step machine. The terminal record
is appended to the existing logical history without erasing, cancelling
or changing any earlier frame, displaced value, effect-tape entry or
open activation. No subsequent source effect occurs, in this activation
or any caller. Forward failure does not reverse the prefix; an eligible
`bennett_reverse` removes the fault record and reverses that intact
prefix under §7.4, §10 and T4.

A Q region never produces a fault record: an oracle's fault is an
outcome in its fault register (§5.2). Its `fault C` is the existing
§4.2 D row: one executed reference row charges one step of B, writes
`code ^= C′; faulted ^= 1`, and selects HALT. Remaining forward machine
steps are the idle steps of §3.6; commit and prefix reversal follow
§5.2, undoing the fault writes and all preceding rows while retaining
the committed fault-register XOR (§14.1, T2). The classical terminal
record and the Q step-machine outcome are distinct reporting paths.

## 5. Regions
### 4.1 Classical control (CC, R)

| Instruction | Form | Semantics |
|---|---|---|
| `const` | `r = const.iN c` | |
| `add sub mul and or xor shl lshr ashr` | `r = op a b` | wrap; shift ≥ N → 0 |
| `udiv urem sdiv srem` | `r = op a b` | divisor 0 or signed MIN/−1: CC trap; R fault record (`FAULT_UB_DIV0` / `FAULT_UB_SDIV_OVERFLOW`, §4.9) |
| `icmp` | `r = icmp.{eq,ne,ult,ule,slt,sle} a b` | |
| `select` | `r = select p a b` | |
| `zext sext trunc` | `r = op.iM a` | |
| `ptrtoint` / `inttoptr` | `r = ptrtoint.iM p` / `p = inttoptr a` | zero-extend/truncate to M / to ptr_width |
| `load` / `store` | `r = load.iN addr` / `store.iN addr v` | N/8 bytes little-endian, unaligned; outside every live allocation: CC trap / R fault record. The suffix is always `.iN` with N ∈ {8, 16, …, 128}: there is no pointer-typed load or store (a pointer is stored as `ptrtoint.iM` with M = ptr_width and reloaded with `inttoptr`) |
| `alloc` / `free` | `p = alloc n` / `free p` | §7.1 |
| `br` | `br ^b(args)` / `br p ^b1(args) ^b2(args)` | parallel block-parameter assignment |
| `call` / `ret` | `results = call @f args` / `ret vals` | arguments and values are bare operands (`r = call @f a b`, `(r1, r2) = call @f a`, `call @f`, `ret v`, `ret`); results bind in order; a parenthesised argument list is ill-formed for `call`; callee tiers per §3.1 |
| `uncall` | `uncall @f (args) (results)` | **segment uncall**, legal exactly under §7.4: the most recent completed call segment of the history is a call of `@f` whose recorded boundary argument and result values equal the current operand values, every frame after it is pure, and it contains, at any nesting depth, no `measure` frame and no `qreg.free` frame whose cleanliness was not verified; otherwise CC trap / R fault `FAULT_UB_TRAP` with no state change beyond the fault record. The segment is reversed by the cancellation-aware history traversal of §7.4 (LIFO, nested segments included; frames already reversed and `uncall` markers skipped) and its frames are marked reversed; the `uncall` itself pushes a frame naming the segment |
| `trap` | `trap` | CC trap; R fault record (`FAULT_UB_TRAP`, §4.9) |
| `fault` | `fault C` | CC and R; literal 0 ≤ C < 2^64; one terminal record with code C for C ≠ 0, else 4 (§5.4); run ends `FAILED`, no successor executes, prefix history retained for reversal (§7.4, §10, §14.1). One classical instruction (§8.2); in an oracle body or lproc, the one-budget-step §4.2 row instead |
| `qreg.new qreg.free qreg.slice qreg.concat qreg.width qreg.id qreg.from_id` | §3.3 | CC and R (§3.3) |
| `measure` | `r = measure q` / `r = measure.iN q` | CC only; Born rule (§14.2); consumes q; `.iN` for a `qreg[*]` (§3.3) |
| `budget.mint` | `tk = budget.mint @o s h st d` | CC and R; a token for the tuple (s, h, st, d) validated for oracle `@o` (§5.4) |
| `effect` | `effect dev.op args` | R only (§7.3); results are written to memory operands |
| `hostcall` | `rlen = hostcall service ptr len` | CC only (§10) |

**Measurement draws.** Supplied draws are consumed FIFO, one per
executed measurement in dynamic execution order (including measurements
of deterministic states); a failing precondition consumes no draw.
Each draw is in [0,1). Enumerate outcomes lexicographically by their
printed bits string, wire 0 first, and select the first outcome whose
cumulative Born weight is **strictly greater** than the draw times the
total weight. Thus an exact CDF tie selects the next nonzero-weight
outcome. Returned integers remain little-endian (wire 0 is bit 0).
This fixes the reference CLI convention; unsupplied draws follow the
Born distribution (§14.2).

**DRAW-ORDER witness**

```json
{"draws":["0.25","0.75"],"integer_order_values":[1,3],"outcomes_bits":["01","11"],"outcomes_values":[2,3],"reversed_draw_values":[3,2]}
```

Prepare two fresh two-wire registers with H on both wires and measure
them in order. Each has weights 1/4 on `00`, `01`, `10`, `11` in that
order. The first draw selects `01` (integer 2) and the second `11`
(integer 3). Reversed draw consumption would give integers 3,2;
integer-ordered outcomes would give 1,3. Both draws test exact CDF ties.

## 17. Decisions deferred to implementation, with defaults

Each item is one an implementer must decide to build; any decision is
conformant provided two implementations agree on the default below until
the item is revised. Items 1–5 are asserted non-semantic.

1. **Binary encoding.** Default: none; §16 text is the only interchange
   form.
2. **Gate-name registry.** Default: the names of §4.4 only; an unknown
   gate name is a load error.
3. **Further device models.** Default: the four of §7.3; an `effect` on
   any other device is `FAULT_EFFECT_UNMODELLED`.
4. **Mechanisation of §14.** Default: none; the reference interpreter is
   the oracle.
5. **Memory layout of the opaque C objects.** Default: opaque; only the
   listed functions touch them.
6. **Measurement-based cleanup** (§6.4): the normative fixup, its
   controlled variant and its certificate kind. Default: unitary cleanup
   (`reverse`); `capabilities.mbu` is `false`.
7. **Expansion library** (§11): the gate-level expansion below row
   level. Default: the library `ref` published by `bennett-isa-spec`
   (ripple-carry adders and comparators, linear `inrange` scans,
   one-hot address dispatch, clean scratch released in reverse order);
   a schedule that names no `expansion` binds `ref` at the ISA version
   in the object's header, with the full-library envelope hash published
   for that version in §11.1. [deps: ISA-9.1, ISA-11.1-P3, ISA-11.1-P4]
   For a 0.9 object this is exactly
   `{"name":"ref","version":"0.9","hash":"5de4e4995350313e50217880ba1b95de39cd043283b726337d4894efb005d197"}`,
   equally for omission and an explicit binding. [deps: ISA-11.1-P3, ISA-11.1-P4, ISA-11.1]
   §11.1 fixes its schema, ordinals, scratch identities, empty removable
   sets and deterministic decomposition algorithms. [deps: ISA-11.1]
   The bound catalog is published as `spec/ref-0.9.json`. The worked
   `ctl` action fixture (zsw.4) is in
   `conformance/fixtures/controlled-action-09/`: `controlled.bisa`,
   `target.json`, `leaves.json`, `schedule.json`, `frame.json` and
   `certificate.json`. It contains one exact controlled-X leaf, starts
   at time 0 and ends at time 1, with two live fast-class interface wires
   and no workspace. Its README records derivation and the pinned 0.8
   compatibility check separately from the 0.9 bindings.
8. **Enclosure procedure for transcendental entries** (§12.1). Default:
   Taylor series of sin and cos to the first term whose Lagrange
   remainder bound is below 2^−(`precision_bits` + 2), argument reduced
   modulo 2π with an outward-rounded enclosure of π.
9. **Default `epsilon_share`** (§8.1, §11) when a schedule assigns none.
   Default: the exact rational `bindings.epsilon / N`, where N is the
   number of gate operations in the bound-library expansion of one
   invocation of `bindings.region`, including every nested invocation,
   that have no exact native match as defined below. [deps: ISA-8.1, ISA-8.3, ISA-11-P2]
   For this count only, an exact native match is a `gates` entry whose
   opcode, classes, control range and parameters cover the operation
   and whose `epsilon` expression is independent of `eps` and evaluates
   to zero; compute this test before the requested-accuracy filter,
   without selecting an achieved-error entry using the default share. [deps: ISA-8.1, ISA-8.3]
   N counts the gate operations before target scheme expansion and
   includes operations with explicit shares as well as omitted shares;
   the root accuracy is `bindings.epsilon`, not a nested region's
   separately requested accuracy. [deps: ISA-11-P2, ISA-11-P3, ISA-8.2, ISA-8.3]
   Gates with an exact native match take a default share of zero; if
   N=0 all default shares are zero and no division is performed. [deps: ISA-8.1, ISA-8.3]
   The resolved share is represented exactly as a reduced pair (p,q)
   of nonnegative numerator and positive denominator, with zero
   represented by (0,1); it is passed as that rational value to every
   §8.1 comparison, expression and accuracy bucket, without rounding
   to a finite decimal. [deps: ISA-8.1]
   This is a resolved value, not an inserted `epsilon_share` field;
   existing decimal schedule fields and the hashed schedule bytes are
   unchanged by default resolution. [deps: ISA-11-P3, ISA-9.2]
   Explicit shares still supply `eps` by their exact value and the
   §8.1 requested-versus-achieved filter and achieved-error charge
   still apply to every resolved share. [deps: ISA-8.1, ISA-8.3]
   *(non-normative witness)* At `bindings.epsilon: "0.1"`, three gates
   lacking exact native matches give N=3 and default (1,30), even if
   one gate has an explicit share; a fourth, exact-native gate takes

1625: ### 7.3 Device models and tapes (R)
1626: 
1627: The active effect tape is specified here; persistence and replay of saved execution journals are tool interfaces, whose interchange format and validation behavior are defined by an explicitly versioned replay profile.
1628: 
1629: Every device operation takes classical-memory operands and writes its
1630: results to memory; each `effect` is **atomic**: validated (operands in
1631: range, tape available) before any change, and a failing validation is a
1632: fault record with no partial delta.
1633: 
1634: | Device | State (initial) | Operations | Failure | Frame records / reversal |
1635: |---|---|---|---|---|
1636: | `console` | byte sequence (empty) | `write(ptr, len)` | – | `len`; pop |
1637: | `clock` | `c: u64` (0) | `read(ptr)`: writes c (8 bytes LE) at ptr, then c += 1 | – | overwritten 8 bytes; c −= 1 |
1638: | `entropy` | position `p` (0) into the entropy tape (§10) | `read(ptr, len)`: copies tape[p..p+len) to ptr; p += len | p + len > length: fault `FAULT_ENTROPY_EXHAUSTED` | overwritten bytes; p −= len |
1639: | `block` | sector map from the `block_image` (else empty), 512-byte sectors; absent sectors read as zero | `read(sector, ptr)`; `write(sector, ptr)` | – | read: overwritten 512 bytes; write: the old sector contents **and a presence bit** (absent sectors are removed again on reversal) |
1640: 
1641: **BLOCK-IMAGE** (K10; source: bennett-vm-47i). `layout.block_image`
2178: 
2179: **States.** `CREATED → RUNNING → (SUSPENDED ⇄ RUNNING) → (HOST_REQUEST ⇄
2180: RUNNING) → COMPLETED | FAILED`; `COMPLETED → REVERSED`; `FAILED →
2181: REVERSED`; any of
2182: `COMPLETED/FAILED/REVERSED → CLOSED` (`bennett_run_close` releases the
2183: record; register ids created by the run remain valid unless measured or
2184: freed). A run owns the registers and memory it touches exclusively while
2185: not CLOSED; a register reached by `qreg.from_id` is touched from then
2186: on; `bennett_qreg_prepare`, `bennett_qreg_free` and a `bennett_run`
2187: argument naming a register owned by another run that is not CLOSED are
2188: `E_INVALID_STATE`. **Reverse eligibility**: `COMPLETED` or `FAILED`; no `measure`
2189: frame; no `qreg.free` frame whose cleanliness was not verified (§17
2190: item 14); no register it touched has been prepared, freed, sliced or
2191: used by another run since; else `E_NOT_REVERSIBLE`. Reversing a `FAILED`
2192: run first undoes its terminal fault record, then traverses the rest of
2193: its history exactly as for a completed run, including the frames of
2194: activations that never returned; the run is then `REVERSED` with every
2195: component T4 names at its entry value. `bennett_qreg_prepare` and `bennett_
2196: qreg_free` require a clean register (`E_QREG_NOT_CLEAN` otherwise on a
2197: verifying executor; a non-verifying one: §17 item 14); there
2198: is no reset and no trace-out: a register in unknown state is released by
2199: a `measure` inside a run, which consumes its handle (§3.3).
